```
      "url": "https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.00801",
      "publishedDate": "2026-04-01T00:00:00.000Z",
      "text": "Routing-Free Mixture-of-Experts\n\n# Routing-Free Mixture-of-Experts\n\nYilun Liu∗,†,1,3, Jinru Han∗,2, Sikuan Yan1, Volker Tresp1,3, Yunpu Ma†,1,3 1 Ludwig Maximilian University of Munich 2 University of California, Los Angeles 3 Munich Center for Machine Learning ∗ Equal contribution. † yilun.liu@tum.de cognitive.yunpu@gmail.com\n\n###### Abstract\n\nStandard Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models rely on centralized routing mechanisms that introduce rigid inductive biases. We propose Routing-Free MoE which eliminates any hard-coded centralized designs including external routers, Softmax, TopK and load balancing, instead encapsulating all activation functionalities within individual experts and are directly optimized through continuous gradient flow, enabling each expert to determine its activation entirely on its own. We introduce a unified adaptive load-balancing framework to simultaneously optimize both expert-balancing and token-balancing objectives through a configurable interpolation, allowing flexible and customizable resource allocation. Extensive experiments show that Routing-Free MoE can consistently outperform baselines with better scalability and robustness. We analyze its behavior in detail and offer insights that may facilitate future MoE design and optimization. Code is available at https://github.com/liuyilun2000/RoutingFreeMoE/tree/release.\n\nFigure 1: Standard MoE relies on routing to orchestrate expert activations. Routing-Free MoE let each expert purely-independently determine its own activation. Green indicates activated components; red for inactive components; yellow for trainable components.\n\n## 1 Introduction\n\nThe scalability of transformer-based Large Language Models (LLMs) (Vaswani et al., 2017) is constrained by the substantial computational resources required (Kaplan et al., 2020). To efficiently expand model capacity without proportional computation cost growth, Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) designs focus on activating subsets of parameters for each input (Shazeer et al., 2017; Fedus et al., 2022). This approach presents a fundamental challenge: how to optimally distribute inputs to experts while satisfying sparsity and balancing considerations.\n\nExisting MoE designs are hindered by structural limitations across multiple dimensions. Standard MoE relies on small, external routers that lack sufficient capacity for storing expert capabilities to determine expert preference for each input, forcing them to learn their prediction via indirect trial-and-error optimization, which inevitably leads to suboptimal routing and unstable early training dynamics (Lv et al., 2025). For computational efficiency, conventional MoE enforces rigid, global constraints on expert activation that ignore input-specific dynamics. The fixed TopK selection imposes uniform sparsity, regardless of varying input complexity and expertise of experts, overlooking potential gains from fewer or more activations (Zhou et al., 2022).       "text": "[physics/0004057] 1 Introduction\n\nThe information bottleneck method\n\nNaftali Tishby,1,2 Fernando C. Pereira,3 and William Bialek1\n\n1NEC Research Institute, 4 Independence Way\n\nPrinceton, New Jersey 08540\n\n2Institute for Computer Science, and\n\nCenter for Neural Computation\n\nHebrew University\n\nJerusalem 91904, Israel\n\n3AT&T Shannon Laboratory\n\n180 Park Avenue\n\nFlorham Park, New Jersey 07932\n\n30 September 1999\n\nWe define the relevant information in a signal $x\\in X$ as being the information that this signal provides about another signal $y\\in{Y}$ . Examples include the information that face images provide about the names of the people portrayed, or the information that speech sounds provide about the words spoken. Understanding the signal $x$ requires more than just predicting $y$ , it also requires specifying which features of ${X}$ play a role in the prediction. We formalize this problem as that of finding a short code for ${X}$ that preserves the maximum information about ${Y}$ . That is, we squeeze the information that ${X}$ provides about ${Y}$ through a ‘bottleneck’ formed by a limited set of codewords ${\\tilde{X}}$ . This constrained optimization problem can be seen as a generalization of rate distortion theory in which the distortion measure $d(x,{\\tilde{x}})$ emerges from the joint statistics of ${X}$ and ${Y}$ . This approach yields an exact set of self consistent equations for the coding rules $X\\rightarrow{\\tilde{X}}$ and ${\\tilde{X}}\\rightarrow{Y}$ . Solutions to these equations can be found by a convergent re–estimation method that generalizes the Blahut–Arimoto algorithm. Our variational principle provides a surprisingly rich framework for discussing a variety of problems in signal processing and learning, as will be described in detail elsewhere.\n\n## 1 Introduction\n\nA fundamental problem in formalizing our intuitive ideas about information is to provide a quantitative notion of “meaningful” or “relevant” information. These issues were intentionally left out of information theory in its original formulation by Shannon, who focused attention on the problem of transmitting information rather than judging its value to the recipient. Correspondingly, information theory has often been viewed as being strictly a theory of communication, and this view has become so accepted that many people consider statistical and information theoretic principles as almost irrelevant for the question of meaning. In contrast, we argue here that information theory, in particular lossy source compression, provides a natural quantitative approach to the question of “relevant information.” Specifically, we formulate a variational principle for the extraction or efficient representation of relevant information. In related work [1] we argue that this single information theoretic principle contains as special cases a wide variety of problems, including prediction, filtering, and learning in its various forms.\n\nThe problem of extracting a relevant summary of data, a",

{
```

```
      "id": "https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.04791",
      "title": "Timer-S1: A Billion-Scale Time Series Foundation Model with Serial ...",
      "url": "https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.04791",
      "publishedDate": "2026-03-05T00:00:00.000Z",
      "text": "Timer-S1: A Billion-Scale Time Series Foundation Model with Serial Scaling\n\n\\contribution\n\n[*]Equal contribution 1]Tsinghua University 2]ByteDance \\contribution[†]Corresponding author\n\n# Timer-S1: A Billion-Scale Time Series Foundation Model with Serial Scaling\n\nYong Liu Xingjian Su Shiyu Wang Haoran Zhang Haixuan Liu Yuxuan Wang Zhou Ye Yang Xiang Jianmin Wang Mingsheng Long [ [mingsheng@tsinghua.edu.cn\n\n(April 9, 2026)\n\n###### Abstract\n\nWe introduce Timer-S1, a strong Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) time series foundation model with 8.3B total parameters, 0.75B activated parameters for each token, and a context length of 11.5K. To overcome the scalability bottleneck in existing pre-trained time series foundation models, we perform Serial Scaling in three dimensions: model architecture, dataset, and training pipeline. Timer-S1 integrates sparse TimeMoE blocks and generic TimeSTP blocks for Serial-Token Prediction (STP), a generic training objective that adheres to the serial nature of forecasting. The proposed paradigm introduces serial computations to improve long-term predictions while avoiding costly rolling-style inference and pronounced error accumulation in the standard next-token prediction. Pursuing a high-quality and unbiased training dataset, we curate TimeBench, a corpus with one trillion time points, and apply meticulous data augmentation to mitigate predictive bias. We further pioneer a post-training stage, including continued pre-training and long-context extension, to enhance short-term and long-context performance. Evaluated on the large-scale GIFT-Eval leaderboard, Timer-S1 achieves state-of-the-art forecasting performance, attaining the best MASE and CRPS scores as a pre-trained model. Timer-S1 is released to facilitate further research.\n\n\\correspondence\n\nMingsheng Long at \\checkdata[Model] https://huggingface.co/bytedance-research/Timer-S1\n\n## 1 Introduction\n\nTime series serve as a fundamental resource across a wide spectrum of real-world applications [friedman1962interpolation, box2013box, hyndman2018forecasting, breunig2000lof, kendall1953analysis], ranging from industrial sensing and financial assessment to healthcare monitoring and climate forecasting. Its inherent capacity to capture dynamic processes and evolving patterns positions it as one of the pivotal modalities on the path toward Artificial General Intelligence (AGI). In recent years, pre-trained time series foundation models have advanced rapidly [das2023decoder, woo2024unified, goswami2024moment, ansari2024chronos, shi2024time], progressively narrowing the gap between model development and deployment [wu2023interpretable, tu2024powerpm, zhang2024trajectory, zhu2025fincast]. The emergence of time series foundation models has initiated a paradigm shift toward General Forecasting, serving as universal forecasters that are free from task-specific training [das2023decoder, cohen2024toto, liu2025sundial, ansari2025chronos, auer2025tirex] and enabling agentic systems to reason",
      "image": "https://arxiv.org/html/2603.04791v3/x1.png",

      "title": "Paper page - Timer-S1: A Billion-Scale Time Series Foundation Model with Serial Scaling",
      "url": "https://hf.co/papers/2603.04791",
      "author": "Yong Liu     ,",
      "text": "Title: Timer-S1: A Billion-Scale Time Series Foundation Model with Serial Scaling\n\nURL Source: https://arxiv.org/html/2603.04791\n\nMarkdown Content:\n\\contribution\n\n[*]Equal contribution 1]Tsinghua University 2]ByteDance \\contribution[†]Corresponding author\n\nXingjian Su Shiyu Wang Haoran Zhang Haixuan Liu Yuxuan Wang Zhou Ye Yang Xiang Jianmin Wang Mingsheng Long [ [ mingsheng@tsinghua.edu.cn\n\n(March 5, 2026)\n\n###### Abstract\n\nWe introduce Timer-S1, a strong Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) time series foundation model with 8.3B total parameters, 0.75B activated parameters for each token, and a context length of 11.5K. To overcome the scalability bottleneck in existing pre-trained time series foundation models, we perform Serial Scaling in three dimensions: model architecture, dataset, and training pipeline. Timer-S1 integrates sparse TimeMoE blocks and generic TimeSTP blocks for Serial-Token Prediction (STP), a generic training objective that adheres to the serial nature of forecasting. The proposed paradigm introduces serial computations to improve long‑term predictions while avoiding costly rolling-style inference and pronounced error accumulation in the standard next-token prediction. Pursuing a high-quality and unbiased training dataset, we curate TimeBench, a corpus with one trillion time points, and apply meticulous data augmentation to mitigate predictive bias. We further pioneer a post-training stage, including continued pre-training and long-context extension, to enhance short-term and long-context performance. Evaluated on the large-scale GIFT-Eval leaderboard, Timer-S1 achieves state-of-the-art forecasting performance, attaining the best MASE and CRPS scores as a pre-trained model. Timer-S1 will be released to facilitate further research.\n\n\\correspondence\n\nMingsheng Long at\n\n###### Contents\n\n1. 1 Introduction\n2. 2 Background\n3. 3 Timer-S1: Architecture\n1. 3.1 Normalization and Embedding\n2. 3.2 Transformer Backbone\n3. 3.3 Forecasting Head\n4. 4 Training\n1. 4.1 Data\n2. 4.2 Pre-Training\n3. 4.3 Post-Training\n4. 4.4 Training Infrastructure\n5. 5 Experiments\n1. 5.1 Benchmark Results\n2. 5.2 Scaling Analysis\n3. 5.3 Ablation Study\n6. 6 Conclusion\n7. References\n\n## 1 Introduction\n\nTime series serve as a fundamental resource across a wide spectrum of real-world applications [friedman1962interpolation, box2013box, hyndman2018forecasting, breunig2000lof, kendall1953analysis], ranging from industrial sensing and financial assessment to healthcare monitoring and climate forecasting. Its inherent capacity to capture dynamic processes and evolving patterns positions it as one of the pivotal modalities on the path toward Artificial General Intelligence (AGI). In recent years, pre-trained time series foundation models have advanced rapidly [das2023decoder, woo2024unified, goswami2024moment, ansari2024chronos, shi2024time], progressively narrowing the gap between model development and deployment [wu2023interpretable, tu2024powerpm, zhang2024trajectory, zhu2025fincas",

      "text": "Timer-S1: Billion-Scale Time Series Forecasting with Serial Scaling — TSFM.ai\n\n---\n\nTsinghua University's Timer model family has grown steadily since 2024, with each generation attacking a different bottleneck in time series foundation models. The original Timer(ICML 2024) established the single-series sequence format and decoder-only pretraining. Timer-XL(ICLR 2025) introduced long-context multi-dimensional attention. Sundial(ICML 2025 Oral) brought flow-matching diffusion and generative probabilistic forecasting. Now, Timer-S1(Liu, Su, Wang et al., March 2026) targets the scalability bottleneck head-on: how to push a time series model past the billion-parameter mark and actually improve forecasting, not just increase model size.\n\nThe result is a sparse Mixture-of-Experts model with 8.3 billion total parameters, 0.75 billion activated per token, and a context length of 11,520 time steps. Evaluated on the GIFT-Eval leaderboard — one of the most comprehensive TSFM benchmarks — Timer-S1 achieves the best MASE (0.693) and CRPS (0.485) scores among pre-trained models. The authors are from Tsinghua University and ByteDance, led by corresponding author Mingsheng Long.\n\n## #Why Scaling Has Been Hard for TSFMs\n\nThere is a well-documented \"scaling bottleneck\" in time series foundation models. In natural language, scaling to billions of parameters with standard next-token prediction reliably improves capability. In time series, prior attempts to apply the same pattern — larger transformers with mixture-of-experts or simply more layers — have yielded diminishing returns or even inferior performance.\n\nThe Timer-S1 authors argue the root cause is a mismatch between training objectives and the nature of forecasting. Time series forecasting is inherently serial: each future value depends on all preceding estimates, and uncertainty compounds step by step. Current approaches sit on two ends of a spectrum:\n\nParallel forecasting models like Moirai predict multiple future steps simultaneously in a single forward pass. This is fast, but the model never explicitly reasons about dependencies between successive forecast steps. It allocates the same amount of computation to the first predicted step and the hundredth, even though the hundredth carries far more accumulated uncertainty.\n\nAutoregressive next-token prediction (NTP) — used by Chronos, TimesFM, and the original Timer — mirrors the serial nature of the task perfectly. Each step is predicted conditioned on all previous steps. But iterative rolling inference is expensive: to produce a 272-step forecast with patch size 16, the model must roll 17 times, passing through the entire backbone each time. Worse, each step's error feeds into the next, causing error accumulation that is far more severe for time series than for text, because time series lack the self-correcting structure of language.\n\nTimer-S1 introduces a third paradigm — Serial Token Prediction (STP) — that introduces serial computations for long-horizon a",
```

```
      "text": "On the Role of Reversible Instance Normalization\n\n\\keepXColumns\n\n# On the Role of Reversible Instance Normalization\n\nGaspard Berthelier EDF R&D & Inria Sophia-Antipolis gaspard.berthelier@edf.fr &Tahar Nabil EDF R&D tahar.nabil@edf.fr &Etienne Le Naour EDF R&D etienne.le-naour@edf.fr Richard Niamke EDF R&D richard.niamke@edf.fr &Samir Perlaza Inria Sophia-Antipolis samir.perlaza@inria.fr &Giovanni Neglia Inria Sophia-Antipolis giovanni.neglia@inria.fr\n\n(October 2025)\n\n###### Abstract\n\nData normalization is a crucial component of deep learning models, yet its role in time series forecasting remains insufficiently understood. In this paper, we identify three central challenges for normalization in time series forecasting: temporal input distribution shift, spatial input distribution shift, and conditional output distribution shift. In this context, we revisit the widely used Reversible Instance Normalization (RevIN), by showing through ablation studies that several of its components are redundant or even detrimental. Based on these observations, we draw new perspectives to improve RevIN’s robustness and generalization.\n\n## 1 Introduction\n\nData normalization is a fundamental preprocessing step, long recognized as essential for the stable and efficient training of neural networks. Its significance was established in early studies on network optimization (LeCun et al., 2012). Normalization is particularly critical in time series forecasting, a domain in which deep learning models have achieved state-of-the-art performance (Nie et al., 2023). Intrinsic properties of time series, such as non-stationarity, trends, and seasonality, introduce challenges that standard normalization methods fail to address (Ogasawara et al., 2010).\n\nThree major aspects must be considered when designing a normalization strategy for time series forecasting (formalized in Appendix˜A):\n\n(i)\n\nTemporal distribution shift. When training a neural forecaster over a training period, the input data distribution in a future test period may differ substantially. For instance, electricity consumption or road traffic often increase over time (see Fig.˜1(a)).\n\n(ii)\n\nSpatial distribution shift. Neural forecasters are typically trained on multiple time series and must generalize to unseen time series at inference. Even when series describe similar phenomena (e.g., solar power generation at different locations), their overall distributions may differ, for example, due to scale or level shifts (see Fig.˜1(b)).\n\n(iii)\n\nConditional distribution shift. A neural forecaster maps past look-back windows to future horizon windows. The conditional distribution of the horizon given the look-back may vary both in space and time. This shift remains particularly challenging to handle (see Fig.˜1(c)).\n\n1. J Additional figures\n\nFigure 1: Illustration of the three distribution shifts: (a) temporal shift between training and test periods (rolling average of a Traffic sensor), (b) spatial shift between users (tw",

      "id": "https://gist.science/paper/2603.11869",
      "title": "On the Role of Reversible Instance Normalization — Plain-Language Explanation | Gist.Science",

      "publishedDate": "2026-03-13T10:32:40.000Z",
      "text": "# On the Role of Reversible Instance Normalization\n\nAuthors: Gaspard Berthelier, Tahar Nabil, Etienne Le Naour, Richard Niamke, Samir Perlaza, Giovanni Neglia\n\nPublished: 2026-03-13\n\narXiv: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.11869\n\nCategories: cs.LG\n\n## Summary\n\nThis paper investigates the role of normalization in time series forecasting by identifying three key distribution shift challenges and demonstrating through ablation studies that the widely used Reversible Instance Normalization (RevIN) contains redundant or detrimental components, thereby proposing new perspectives to enhance its robustness and generalization.\n\n## ELI5\n\nImagine you are trying to teach a robot to predict the future based on patterns it sees today. Maybe it's predicting electricity usage for a city, traffic flow on a highway, or how much solar power a panel will generate tomorrow.\n\nThis paper is about a specific tool the robot uses to \"clean up\" the data before it starts learning. The tool is called Reversible Instance Normalization (RevIN). It's currently the \"gold standard\" in the industry, but the authors of this paper decided to take it apart, like a mechanic inspecting a popular car engine, to see if every single part is actually necessary.\n\nHere is the breakdown in simple terms:\n\n### 1. The Problem: The \"Moving Target\"\n\nTime series data (like weather or stock prices) is tricky because it never stays the same. The authors identify three ways the data changes, which confuse the robot:\n\n- Temporal Shift (Time Travel): The world changes over time. If you train your robot in 2020, the traffic patterns in 2024 might be totally different because of a new highway or a pandemic. It's like trying to teach someone to drive in a quiet village, then sending them to a chaotic city.\n- Spatial Shift (Different Locations): Even if the time is the same, different places act differently. Solar panels in sunny California behave differently than those in cloudy Seattle. The robot needs to learn the pattern, not just the specific numbers for one location.\n- Conditional Shift (The \"Context\" Trap): This is the sneaky one. Sometimes, the same input leads to a different output depending on the context. Imagine a traffic light. If it's red, cars stop. But if it's a parade, cars might stop even if the light is green. The robot needs to understand that the relationship between \"what happened before\" and \"what happens next\" can change.\n\n### 2. The Old Tool: RevIN (The \"Standardized Uniform\")\n\nTo handle these changes, researchers use RevIN. Think of RevIN as a uniform that every piece of data wears before entering the robot's brain.\n\n- How it works: It takes a chunk of data (like the last 24 hours of traffic), measures its average speed and how much it varies, and then forces it to look \"normal\" (average = 0, variation = 1).\n- The Reversible Part: After the robot makes a prediction, RevIN takes the \"uniform\" off and puts the original scale back on, so the prediction makes sense in the real world (e.g., \"",
```

```
      "text": "MoE Myths, Context Compression, and Steering Proofs | Awesome Agents\n\nThree papers landed this week with results that should make practitioners stop and reconsider some deeply held assumptions. One dismantles a central belief about how Mixture of Experts models actually route tokens. Another shows that LLMs can learn to compress their own reasoning traces - cutting KV cache use by more than half. The third proves, with mathematics, that activation steering and prompt-based control are formally distinct interventions.\n\nTL;DR\n\n- The Myth of Expert Specialization in MoEs - Expert routing is driven by representation geometry, not architecture design, and experts aren't as specialized as assumed\n- MEMENTO - LLMs trained to compress their own reasoning into dense summaries cut peak KV cache 2.5x and nearly double inference throughput\n- Steered LLM Activations are Non-Surjective - Activation steering pushes models off the manifold reachable by any prompt, making the two intervention types formally incomparable\n\n## The MoE Routing Story Doesn't Hold Up\n\nPaper: \"The Myth of Expert Specialization in Mixture of Experts\" by Xi Wang, Soufiane Hayou, and Eric Nalisnick. ArXiv:2604.09780.\n\nMixture of Experts models - used in Mixtral, DeepSeek V3, and a growing share of frontier systems - are built around a seductive idea: different experts specialize in different types of content. The router learns to send math tokens to the math expert, code tokens to the code expert, and so on. It's clean, intuitive, and mostly wrong.\n\nWang, Hayou, and Nalisnick show that expert specialization in MoE models isn't a product of the routing architecture at all. It's an emergent consequence of the representation space geometry. With linear routers, hidden state similarity becomes both necessary and sufficient to explain which expert a token gets sent to. The router isn't learning semantic categories; it's reflecting the geometry of the embeddings it receives.\n\nHow a Switch Transformer-style gating network dispatches tokens to experts - the geometry of embeddings, not learned semantic categories, drives these choices. Source: huggingface.co\n\nThe load-balancing loss that most MoE implementations use actually suppresses shared directions in the representation space to keep routing varied.       "url": "https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.25262",
```
```
      "text": "IBNorm: Information-Bottleneck Inspired Normalization for Representation Learning\n\n# IBNorm: Information-Bottleneck Inspired Normalization for Representation Learning\n\nXiandong Zou Jia Li Xiaotong Yuan Pan Zhou\n\n###### Abstract\n\nNormalization is fundamental to deep learning, but existing approaches such as BatchNorm, LayerNorm, and RMSNorm are variance-centric by enforcing zero mean and unit variance, stabilizing training without controlling how representations capture task-relevant information. We propose IB-Inspired Normalization (IBNorm), a simple yet powerful family of methods grounded in the Information Bottleneck principle. IBNorm introduces bounded compression operations that encourage embeddings to preserve predictive information while suppressing nuisance variability, yielding more informative representations while retaining the stability and compatibility of standard normalization. Theoretically, we prove that IBNorm achieves a higher IB value and tighter generalization bounds than variance-centric methods. Empirically, IBNorm outperforms BatchNorm, LayerNorm, and RMSNorm across large-scale language models (like LLaMA, GPT-2) and vision models (e.g., ResNet, ViT), with mutual information analysis confirming superior information bottleneck behavior. Code will be released publicly and is available in the supplementary now.\n\nMachine Learning, ICML\n\n## 1 Introduction\n\nNormalization has become a cornerstone of deep learning, credited for stabilizing and accelerating training across domains. Techniques such as Batch Normalization (BN) (Ioffe and Szegedy, 2015), Layer Normalization (LN) (Ba et al., 2016), and RMSNorm (Zhang and Sennrich, 2019) are now standard in architectures spanning vision and language (Vaswani et al., 2017; Dosovitskiy et al., 2020; He et al., 2016b), underscoring normalization as a key driver of training stability and acceleration.\n\nDespite this success, existing normalization methods share a fundamental limitation: they are inherently variance-centric. By enforcing zero mean and unit variance, followed by rescaling and shifting, they improve the conditioning of the optimization problem (Huang et al., 2023, 2018; He et al., 2016a; Desjardins et al., 2015). However, this process operates purely on first- and second-order statistics, without offering any guidance on (intermediate) representation learning. Indeed, two representations may share identical mean and variance, but can encode drastically different amounts of task-relevant information (Raginsky et al., 2016; Achille and Soatto, 2018b) This raises a key question: can normalization be re-designed not only to stabilize training but also to shape representations toward sufficiency and generalization?\n\nPrior works have explored this direction. For instance, NormalNorm (Eftekhari and Papyan, 2025) encourages Gaussian-like features via power transforms and additive noise, arguing that maximizing feature entropy improves representation learning. We argue that this objectiv",
```

```
      "id": "https://mlanthology.org/neurips/2022/chi2022neurips-representation/",
      "title": "On the Representation Collapse of Sparse Mixture of Experts | ML Anthology",
      "url": "https://mlanthology.org/neurips/2022/chi2022neurips-representation/",
      "text": "On the Representation Collapse of Sparse Mixture of Experts | ML Anthology\n\n# On the Representation Collapse of Sparse Mixture of Experts\n\n## Abstract\n\nSparse mixture of experts provides larger model capacity while requiring a constant computational overhead. It employs the routing mechanism to distribute input tokens to the best-matched experts according to their hidden representations. However, learning such a routing mechanism encourages token clustering around expert centroids, implying a trend toward representation collapse. In this work, we propose to estimate the routing scores between tokens and experts on a low-dimensional hypersphere. We conduct extensive experiments on cross-lingual language model pre-training and fine-tuning on downstream tasks. Experimental results across seven multilingual benchmarks show that our method achieves consistent gains. We also present a comprehensive analysis on the representation and routing behaviors of our models. Our method alleviates the representation collapse issue and achieves more consistent routing than the baseline mixture-of-experts methods.\n\n## Cite\n\n### Text\n\nCopy\n\n```\nChi et al. \"On the Representation Collapse of Sparse Mixture of Experts.\" Neural Information Processing Systems, 2022.\n```\n\n### Markdown\n\nCopy\n\n```\n[Chi et al. \"On the Representation Collapse of Sparse Mixture of Experts.\" Neural Information Processing Systems, 2022.](https://mlanthology.org/neurips/2022/chi2022neurips-representation/)\n```\n\n### BibTeX\n\nCopy\n\n```\n@inproceedings{chi2022neurips-representation,\n  title     = {{On the Representation Collapse of Sparse Mixture of Experts}},\n  author    = {Chi, Zewen and Dong, Li and Huang, Shaohan and Dai, Damai and Ma, Shuming and Patra, Barun and Singhal, Saksham and Bajaj, Payal and Song, Xia and Mao, Xian-Ling and Huang, Heyan and Wei, Furu},\n  booktitle = {Neural Information Processing Systems},\n  year      = {2022},\n  url       = {https://mlanthology.org/neurips/2022/chi2022neurips-representation/}\n}\n```"
```

```
      "text": "Input Domain Aware MoE: Decoupling Routing Decisions from Task Optimization in Mixture of Experts\n\n# Input Domain Aware MoE: Decoupling Routing Decisions from Task Optimization in Mixture of Experts\n\nYongXiang Hua https://orcid.org/0009-0008-8849-4717 University of Science and Technology of ChinaState Key Laboratory of Cognitive IntelligenceHefeiChina yx15333063290@mail.ustc.edu.cn, Haoyu Cao https://orcid.org/0000-0002-3789-9705 University of Science and Technology of ChinaState Key Laboratory of Cognitive IntelligenceHefeiChina caohaoyu@mail.ustc.edu.cn, Zhou Tao https://orcid.org/0009-0005-4433-4373 University of Science and Technology of ChinaState Key Laboratory of Cognitive IntelligenceHefeiChina zhoutao24@mail.ustc.edu.cn, Bocheng Li https://orcid.org/0009-0005-2171-7902 University of Science and Technology of ChinaState Key Laboratory of Cognitive IntelligenceHefeiChina bcli@mail.ustc.edu.cn, Zihao Wu https://orcid.org/0009-0007-2199-8518 University of Science and Technology of ChinaState Key Laboratory of Cognitive IntelligenceHefeiChina zihaowu@mail.ustc.edu.cn, Chaohu Liu https://orcid.org/0009-0001-7588-4264 University of Science and Technology of ChinaState Key Laboratory of Cognitive IntelligenceHefeiChina liuchaohu@mail.ustc.edu.cn and Linli Xu https://orcid.org/0000-0003-0227-3793 University of Science and Technology of ChinaState Key Laboratory of Cognitive IntelligenceHefeiChina linlixu@ustc.edu.cn\n\n(2025)\n\n###### Abstract.\n\nSparse Mixture of Experts (sMoE) has become a pivotal approach for scaling large vision-language models, offering substantial capacity while maintaining computational efficiency through dynamic, sparse activation of experts. However, existing routing mechanisms, typically based on similarity scoring, struggle to effectively capture the underlying input structure. This limitation leads to a trade-off between expert specialization and balanced computation, hindering both scalability and performance. We propose Input Domain Aware MoE, a novel routing framework that leverages a probabilistic mixture model to better partition the input space. By modeling routing probabilities as a mixture of distributions, our method enables experts to develop clear specialization boundaries while achieving balanced utilization. Unlike conventional approaches, our routing mechanism is trained independently of task-specific objectives, allowing for stable optimization and decisive expert assignments. Empirical results on vision-language tasks demonstrate that our method consistently outperforms existing sMoE approaches, achieving higher task performance and improved expert utilization balance.\n\nLarge Multimodal Model, Visual Question Answering, Mixture of Experts, Load Balancing\n\n††journalyear: 2025††copyright: acmlicensed††conference: Proceedings of the 33rd ACM International Conference on Multimedia; October 27–31, 2025; Dublin, Ireland††booktitle: Proceedings of the 33rd ACM International Conference on Multimedia (MM ’25), Octo",
```

```
      "id": "http://aclanthology.org/D19-1410/",
      "title": "Sentence-BERT: Sentence Embeddings using Siamese BERT-Networks - ACL Anthology",
      "url": "http://aclanthology.org/D19-1410/",
      "text": "Sentence-BERT: Sentence Embeddings using Siamese BERT-Networks - ACL Anthology\n\nNils Reimers, Iryna Gurevych\n\n---\n\n##### Abstract\n\nBERT (Devlin et al., 2018) and RoBERTa (Liu et al., 2019) has set a new state-of-the-art performance on sentence-pair regression tasks like semantic textual similarity (STS). However, it requires that both sentences are fed into the network, which causes a massive computational overhead: Finding the most similar pair in a collection of 10,000 sentences requires about 50 million inference computations (~65 hours) with BERT. The construction of BERT makes it unsuitable for semantic similarity search as well as for unsupervised tasks like clustering. In this publication, we present Sentence-BERT (SBERT), a modification of the pretrained BERT network that use siamese and triplet network structures to derive semantically meaningful sentence embeddings that can be compared using cosine-similarity. This reduces the effort for finding the most similar pair from 65 hours with BERT / RoBERTa to about 5 seconds with SBERT, while maintaining the accuracy from BERT. We evaluate SBERT and SRoBERTa on common STS tasks and transfer learning tasks, where it outperforms other state-of-the-art sentence embeddings methods.\n\nAnthology ID:D19-1410Volume: Proceedings of the 2019 Conference on Empirical Methods in Natural Language Processing and the 9th International Joint Conference on Natural Language Processing (EMNLP-IJCNLP) Month:NovemberYear:2019Address:Hong Kong, ChinaEditors: Kentaro Inui, Jing Jiang, Vincent Ng, Xiaojun Wan Venues: EMNLP| IJCNLP SIG: SIGDAT Publisher:Association for Computational LinguisticsNote:Pages:3982–3992Language:URL: https://aclanthology.org/D19-1410/ DOI: 10.18653/v1/D19-1410 Bibkey:REDACTED (ACL):Nils Reimers and Iryna Gurevych. 2019.  (57, \"reimers2019sentencebert\",\"Reimers\",    2019, \"EMNLP 2019\",          \"FOUND\", \"https://aclanthology.org/D19-1410/\", \"academic_conf\", \"EMNLP-IJCNLP 2019, pp.3982-3992\"),\n]\n\nEXPECTED_COUNT = 57\n\ndef main():\n    wb = Workbook()\n    ws = wb.active\n    ws.title = \"Bib Verification\"\n\n    headers = [\"#\", \"BibKey\", \"First Author\", \"Year (cite)\", \"Claimed Venue\", \"Status\",\n               \"Verified URL\", \"Source Kind\", \"Notes\"]\n    ws.append(headers)\n\n    header_font = Font(bold=True, color=\"FFFFFF\")\n    header_fill = PatternFill(\"solid\", fgColor=\"305496\")\n    for col in range(1, len(headers) + 1):\n        c = ws.cell(row=1, column=col)\n        c.font = header_font\n        c.fill = header_fill\n        c.alignment = Alignment(horizontal=\"center\", vertical=\"center\")\n\n    bad_fill = PatternFill(\"solid\", fgColor=\"FFD6D6\")\n    warn_fill = PatternFill(\"solid\", fgColor=\"FFF2CC\")\n    ok_fill   = PatternFill(\"solid\", fgColor=\"E2EFDA\")\n\n    for r in ROWS:\n        ws.append(list(r))\n        row_idx = ws.max_row\n        status = r[5]\n        fill = ok_fill if status == \"FOUND\" else (bad_fill if \"MISMATCH\" in status else warn_fill)\n        for col in range(1, len(headers) + 1):\n            ws.cell(row=row_idx, column=col).fill = fill\n\n    # Column widths\n    widths = [4, 28, 14, 11, 24, 18, 70, 28, 90]\n    for i, w in enumerate(widths, start=1):\n        ws.column_dimensions[chr(64 + i) if i <= 26 else \"AA\"].width = w\n\n    # Summary sheet\n    ws2 = wb.create_sheet(\"Summary\")\n    found    = sum(1 for r in ROWS if r[5] == \"FOUND\")\n    aut_mm   = sum(1 for r in ROWS if r[5] == \"AUTHOR_MISMATCH\")\n    aut_inc  = sum(1 for r in ROWS if r[5] == \"AUTHOR_INCOMPLETE\")\n    venue_mm = sum(1 for r in ROWS if r[5] == \"VENUE_MISMATCH\")\n\n    ws2[\"A1\"] = \"Bibliography verification summary\"\n    ws2[\"A1\"].font = Font(bold=True, size=14)\n    ws2.append([])\n    ws2.append([\"Total rows in xlsx\", len(ROWS)])\n    ws2.append([\"Expected count (from \\\\bibitem grep on main.tex)\", EXPECTED_COUNT])\n    ws2.append([\"Counts match\", \"YES\" if len(ROWS) == EXPECTED_COUNT else \"NO\"])\n    ws2.append([])\n    ws2.append([\"FOUND (clean)\", found])\n    ws2.append([\"AUTHOR_MISMATCH (paper exists, wrong authors in bib)\", aut_mm])\n    ws2.append([\"AUTHOR_INCOMPLETE (paper exists, et al. shorthand misses true first author)\", aut_inc])\n    ws2.append([\"VENUE_MISMATCH (paper exists, year/venue off-by-one)\", venue_mm])\n    ws2.append([\"Sum of all categories\", found + aut_mm + aut_inc + venue_mm])\n    ws2.append([])\n    ws2.append([\"All cited papers exist as academic sources?\",\n                \"YES (every entry resolves to a real conference/journal/arXiv preprint/textbook)\"])\n    ws2.append([\"Source kinds present\",\n                \"academic_conf, academic_journal, academic_preprint, academic_book, academic_preprint_and_conf\"])\n\n    ws2.column_dimensions[\"A\"].width = 70\n    ws2.column_dimensions[\"B\"].width = 20\n\n    out = \"/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/bib_verification.xlsx\"\n    wb.save(out)\n    print(f\"Wrote {out}\")\n    print(f\"Rows in xlsx: {len(ROWS)}; expected from grep: {EXPECTED_COUNT}; match: {len(ROWS) == EXPECTED_COUNT}\")\n    print(f\"FOUND={found}, AUTHOR_MISMATCH={aut_mm}, AUTHOR_INCOMPLETE={aut_inc}, VENUE_MISMATCH={venue_mm}\")\n\nif __name__ == \"__main__\":\n    main()\n"
}
```

```
68:This natural mixture-of-experts approach fails on TSFMs that use instance normalization once any backbone layers are unfrozen (Figure~\ref{fig:problem_overview}b). When the standard MoE recipe~\citep{wang2022adamix, fedus2022switch}, routing on hidden states with load balancing, is applied to MOMENT with RevIN, routing entropy drops to zero in most unfrozen configurations (Table~\ref{tab:adamix}). The router learns to send every window to the same expert, and the remaining experts are never used. The mechanism is what we call \emph{normalization-induced routing collapse}: RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin} strips the per-window mean and variance that the router needs to distinguish input regimes, leaving it with homogenized hidden states. Even with the backbone frozen, hidden-state routing still underperforms; with the backbone unfrozen, the dominant expert receives larger gradients which reshape the backbone to serve it even more, closing a co-adaptation feedback loop within tens of training steps (Figure~\ref{fig:trajectory}). The failure is specific to instance normalization: backbones such as Moirai that use LayerNorm instead of RevIN do not collapse (\S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}). Standard MoE rescue mechanisms (load balancing, entropy regularization, z-loss, ReLU routing, expert-choice) also do not help (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}), because the problem lies in the router's \emph{input}, not its optimization.
234:{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Normalization in Time Series.} RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin} mitigates distribution shift; DishTS~\citep{fan2023dishts} and Non-stationary Transformers~\citep{liu2022nonstationary} partially recover stripped statistics. Concurrently, \citet{berthelier2026revin} independently show that several RevIN components are redundant or detrimental, and \citet{zou2025ibnorm} argue that variance-centric normalization (BatchNorm, LayerNorm, RMSNorm) is suboptimal for representation learning because two representations with identical mean and variance can encode different amounts of task-relevant information. We identify a specific, mechanistic consequence of this principle that has not been characterized in the MoE literature: when the stripped statistics carry the \emph{routing} signal in a downstream MoE adapter, instance normalization causes the router to collapse to a single expert under unfreezing, a failure mode distinct from the optimization-induced collapse studied in standard MoE rescue literature.}
387:\textbf{Backbones.} Five backbones spanning two normalization families: instance-normalized MOMENT-small/large~\citep{goswami2024moment} (internal RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin}); LayerNorm-only Moirai~\citep{woo2024moirai} and Moirai-MoE; and Chronos~\citep{ansari2024chronos} and Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl} as no-instance-normalization negative controls (\S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}). MOMENT-small is primary. Primary results use a strictly frozen backbone; freeze-level ablations follow.
789:\bibitem[Kim et~al.(2022)]{kim2021revin}
---
64:Time series foundation models (TSFMs) such as MOMENT~\citep{goswami2024moment}, TimesFM~\citep{das2024timesfm}, Chronos~\citep{ansari2024chronos}, Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl}, and Moirai~\citep{woo2024moirai} now provide powerful pretrained representations for time series, with several recent variants pushing toward billion-parameter scales~\citep{lee2024units, rasul2024lagllama, shi2024timemoe, ekambaram2024ttm, liu2026timers1}. Using one of these backbones on a downstream task still requires attaching a lightweight adapter head that maps hidden states to predictions, and the design of that adapter, more than the backbone choice, determines deployment utility.
724:\bibitem[Gao et~al.(2024)]{lee2024units}
---
232:\textbf{MoE routing collapse and rescue.} Routing collapse has been framed two ways. \emph{Optimization-side}: load-balancing losses~\citep{fedus2022switch}, z-loss~\citep{zoph2022stmoe}, Expert-Choice routing~\citep{zhou2022expertchoice}, ReLU gating~\citep{zhang2025remoe}, and AdaMix's hidden-state router~\citep{wang2022adamix} all attack training dynamics. \emph{Representation-side}: \citet{chi2022representation} attribute collapse to post-training token clustering around expert centroids and propose hyperspherical routing scores, and \citet{hua2025inputaware} explicitly decouple routing decisions from task optimization to better capture input structure. Our setting differs from both: the routing signal is structurally absent from the router's input \emph{by design of an upstream architectural normalizer (RevIN)}, before any training dynamics or post-training feature clustering can act. Sweeping the five optimization-side families across eleven configurations on MOMENT$+$RevIN, none recover MSE to within $2.7{\times}$ of RR-MoA (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}). Concurrent work in NLP/vision toward router-free MoEs (AoE~\citep{lv2025aoe}, ERMoE~\citep{cheng2025ermoe}, Routing-Free MoE~\citep{liu2026routingfree}) supports the broader view that the router's input matters: in our setting (\S\ref{sec:main_results}), self-gated experts on raw input match RR-MoA, while the same architecture on hidden states collapses. \citet{wang2026myth} proves MoE specialization reflects hidden-state geometry; we extend this upstream by identifying what shapes that geometry on TSFMs.
487:\textbf{Rescue-baseline sweep.} To rule out that the collapse is merely under-tuned, we sweep five standard MoE rescue families (Switch load-balance~\citep{fedus2022switch}, ST-MoE z-loss~\citep{zoph2022stmoe}, entropy regularization, ReMoE ReLU routing~\citep{zhang2025remoe}, and expert-choice routing~\citep{zhou2022expertchoice}) across eleven configurations on 6 datasets $\times$ 2 freeze levels $\times$ 5 seeds $= 720$ runs (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}, Appendix~\ref{app:rescue_details}). The best rescue (z-loss $c_z{=}0.01$) reduces MSE by only $10.9\%$, still $2.7{\times}$ worse than RR-MoA. Excessive balancing ($\alpha{=}10.0$) actively hurts MSE ($+67.7\%$). This confirms Observation~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}(ii): when routing information is structurally absent from the hidden states, none of the standard rescue mechanisms we tested can compensate.
794:\bibitem[Wang et~al.(2025)]{zhang2025remoe}
---
228:\textbf{TSFM Adaptation.} The standard recipe attaches a lightweight module to a frozen TSFM and tunes it on downstream data: LoRA~\citep{hu2022lora}, bottleneck adapters~\citep{houlsby2019adapters}, or a linear probe~\citep{goswami2024moment}. Recent variations explore multi-scale finetuning~\citep{qiao2025msft}, importance-based LoRA selection~\citep{li2025trace}, input-representation adaptation~\citep{benechehab2025adapts}, structured pruning~\citep{zhao2025prune}, in-context tuning~\citep{faw2025icf}, and PEFT alternatives for Chronos~\citep{gupta2024beyondlora}; benchmarking work includes TEMPLATE~\citep{zhang2025template} and GIFT-Eval~\citep{woo2024gifteval}. All of these search over finetuning \textit{strategies} while keeping a single adapter architecture. We instead ask whether the adapter topology should be chosen per input window.
839:\bibitem[Aksu et~al.(2024)]{woo2024gifteval}
```
